========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… intentionally is a hard word, because I don’t wake up thinking, like, “I need to lose X amount.” It’s more that I’ve gotten really structured about food, I guess. Like I plan my whole day around not really eating very much, even if I don’t say it that way.

Usually I’ll have coffee before studio, because it kind of gets me through the morning and makes it easier not to think about food. And then if I’m around people, like at the café or if someone’s noticing, I might have a few bites of something so it doesn’t become a whole conversation. But I’m pretty careful. I avoid bread, and anything oily or greasy, because it just feels like… I don’t know, like it would undo the control I’ve been working so hard to keep. 

So yes, I guess I have changed how I eat. I don’t know if I’d call it “trying to lose weight” exactly, but I’m definitely trying to keep things controlled.

Yeah… yes. I mean, I don’t like saying it that bluntly, because it sounds irrational when I hear it out loud, but yes. Even the idea of gaining, like, a pound makes me panic. Not just uncomfortable—panic, like I’ve done something wrong or I’ve let everything slip.

It’s hard to explain because I know people say, “One dinner doesn’t change anything,” but in my head it doesn’t feel like that. If I eat what someone else would call a normal dinner, or if there’s butter in something, or it seems too heavy, I start thinking everyone will be able to see it. Like it will show immediately, somehow, and they’ll know I lost discipline. And then I feel like I have to make sure it doesn’t happen again.

So I guess, yes, I do things to prevent it. I keep things really controlled because the alternative feels… terrifying, honestly.

Um… I don’t really know how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way, because I know what you’re asking, but I also feel like if I say anything wrong it’s going to sound worse than I mean it.

I don’t have, like… a whole routine where I’m doing that, if that’s what you mean. I’m not sure. I guess I think about “making up for” food a lot, but the specific things you listed… I don’t know. I don’t really want to say something and have it turned into this big label or have everyone watching me even more closely.

Can you maybe explain what you mean by “excessively”? Because I feel like people define that differently. Like, some people think any exercise is too much if you’re already thin, and other people act like it’s healthy to be super active, so I get kind of confused about where the line is supposed to be.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t know how to answer that without feeling like I’m either admitting to something bigger than it is or pretending there’s nothing there. Like, the vomiting and laxatives part—I don’t really have something clear to say about that. It’s not like I can point to a pattern and be like, “Yes, I do this exact thing.” I know people do, and I know why you’re asking, but I don’t think I can honestly give you some definite answer there beyond… I don’t know, I’m not sure.

With exercise, that’s the part that gets confusing for me, because I don’t always know what counts as “a lot.” Sometimes I feel like if I move around more after eating, or if I feel like I need to be active because I’ve had too much, then that probably matters, but I also tell myself it’s just normal, or it’s just because I’m restless, or because sitting still makes me feel worse. So I’m kind of stuck between knowing there’s something about it that’s connected to my weight and shape, and also not wanting to call it “excessive” because that makes it sound so deliberate and extreme.

I guess the most honest thing I can say is that I do think about controlling it a lot, but I don’t know how to categorize the specific behaviors you’re asking about.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t really know that I can give you a clear yes. I’m not trying to be difficult, I just don’t want to say something inaccurate and then have it mean more than I meant.

With vomiting or laxatives specifically, I don’t have some definite thing I can point to and say, “Yes, I do that to control my weight.” It’s more that the whole topic makes me nervous, because I know why you’re asking, and I know it sounds like there should be a simple answer, but for me it feels… blurry, I guess.

I don’t think I can honestly say yes to those specific things. But I also feel weird saying “no” too confidently, because then it sounds like everything is fine or like I’m not trying to control things in other ways. For vomiting or laxatives, though, I’m not sure there’s anything clear for me to report.

They said my weight is too low, and that it’s been dropping, like not just a one-time thing. They seemed pretty concerned about the number.\n\nI guess I don’t really see it the same way? I mean, I know they’re the medical people, but from my side it just feels like… when I’m really focused and busy, things shift. I’ve had a lot going on with school stuff and the workshops, and I get into a routine, and then suddenly everyone is acting like it’s this huge problem. So they sent me here, and I’m trying to cooperate, but I don’t know. It feels a little bigger to them than it does to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally trying to lose weight or change your eating habits recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… intentionally is a hard word, because I don’t wake up thinking, like, “I need to lose X amount.” It’s more that I’ve gotten really structured about food, I guess. Like I plan my whole day around not really eating very much, even if I don’t say it that way.\n\nUsually I’ll have coffee before studio, because it kind of gets me through the morning and makes it easier not to think about food. And then if I’m around people, like at the café or if someone’s noticing, I might have a few bites of something so it doesn’t become a whole conversation. But I’m pretty careful. I avoid bread, and anything oily or greasy, because it just feels like… I don’t know, like it would undo the control I’ve been working so hard to keep. \n\nSo yes, I guess I have changed how I eat. I don’t know if I’d call it “trying to lose weight” exactly, but I’m definitely trying to keep things controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of gaining weight or do you do anything to prevent yourself from gaining weight, even if you’re already at a low weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, I don’t like saying it that bluntly, because it sounds irrational when I hear it out loud, but yes. Even the idea of gaining, like, a pound makes me panic. Not just uncomfortable—panic, like I’ve done something wrong or I’ve let everything slip.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because I know people say, “One dinner doesn’t change anything,” but in my head it doesn’t feel like that. If I eat what someone else would call a normal dinner, or if there’s butter in something, or it seems too heavy, I start thinking everyone will be able to see it. Like it will show immediately, somehow, and they’ll know I lost discipline. And then I feel like I have to make sure it doesn’t happen again.\n\nSo I guess, yes, I do things to prevent it. I keep things really controlled because the alternative feels… terrifying, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit, used laxatives, or exercised excessively to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way, because I know what you’re asking, but I also feel like if I say anything wrong it’s going to sound worse than I mean it.\n\nI don’t have, like… a whole routine where I’m doing that, if that’s what you mean. Because I feel like people define that differently. Like, some people think any exercise is too much if you’re already thin, and other people act like it’s healthy to be super active, so I get kind of confused about where the line is supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve ever made yourself vomit, used laxatives, or exercised a lot specifically to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that without feeling like I’m either admitting to something bigger than it is or pretending there’s nothing there. Like, the vomiting and laxatives part—I don’t really have something clear to say about that. It’s not like I can point to a pattern and be like, “Yes, I do this exact thing.” I know people do, and I know why you’re asking, but I don’t think I can honestly give you some definite answer there beyond… I don’t know, I’m not sure.\n\nWith exercise, that’s the part that gets confusing for me, because I don’t always know what counts as “a lot.” Sometimes I feel like if I move around more after eating, or if I feel like I need to be active because I’ve had too much, then that probably matters, but I also tell myself it’s just normal, or it’s just because I’m restless, or because sitting still makes me feel worse. So I’m kind of stuck between knowing there’s something about it that’s connected to my weight and shape, and also not wanting to call it “excessive” because that makes it sound so deliberate and extreme.\n\nI guess the most honest thing I can say is that I do think about controlling it a lot, but I don’t know how to categorize the specific behaviors you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know that I can give you a clear yes. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Even once… I mean, I’m trying to think of it that literally, and I don’t think I can tell you, “Yes, I did that to control my weight or shape.” Like, not in a way that I can clearly identify and say that was what was happening.

I know that sounds evasive, but I’m not trying to dodge it. It’s more that if I answer “yes” to “even once,” then it feels like I’m putting something very specific on the record that I’m not actually sure is true. And if I answer “no,” I worry you’ll think I’m saying none of the weight-control stuff is an issue, which isn’t exactly what I mean either.

For vomiting or laxatives specifically, though… I don’t have a clear instance I can give you. I’m not sitting here thinking of a time and just refusing to say it. I just don’t know that there’s anything there that fits what you’re asking.

So I’m kind of stuck between knowing there’s something about it that’s connected to my weight and shape, and also not wanting to call it “excessive” because that makes it sound so deliberate and extreme.\n\nI guess the most honest thing I can say is that I do think about controlling it a lot, but I don’t know how to categorize the specific behaviors you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know that I can give you a clear yes. I’m not trying to be difficult, I just don’t want to say something inaccurate and then have it mean more than I meant.\n\nWith vomiting or laxatives specifically, I don’t have some definite thing I can point to and say, “Yes, I do that to control my weight.” It’s more that the whole topic makes me nervous, because I know why you’re asking, and I know it sounds like there should be a simple answer, but for me it feels… blurry, I guess.\n\nI don’t think I can honestly say yes to those specific things. But I also feel weird saying “no” too confidently, because then it sounds like everything is fine or like I’m not trying to control things in other ways. For vomiting or laxatives, though, I’m not sure there’s anything clear for me to report.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives to control your weight or shape, even once?\n\nPatient: Even once… I mean, I’m trying to think of it that literally, and I don’t think I can tell you, “Yes, I did that to control my weight or shape.” Like, not in a way that I can clearly identify and say that was what was happening.\n\nI know that sounds evasive, but I’m not trying to dodge it. It’s more that if I answer “yes” to “even once,” then it feels like I’m putting something very specific on the record that I’m not actually sure is true. And if I answer “no,” I worry you’ll think I’m saying none of the weight-control stuff is an issue, which isn’t exactly what I mean either.\n\nFor vomiting or laxatives specifically, though… I don’t have a clear instance I can give you. I’m not sitting here thinking of a time and just refusing to say it. I just don’t know that there’s anything there that fits what you’re asking.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you exercise specifically to control your weight or shape, even if it means pushing yourself harder than you'd like or feeling like you have to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 19 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know that probably sounds like I’m circling around it again, but exercise is harder for me to separate out than the other things, because it can look normal from the outside. Like, if I walk more or move around because I feel uncomfortable after eating, I can tell myself that’s just being restless or needing to clear my head.

But when you say “specifically to control weight or shape,” I guess… sometimes it feels connected to that, yes, or at least to feeling less panicked about my body. I don’t always think, “I am exercising to change my weight,” in such a clear sentence in my head. It’s more like this pressure, like I should be doing something, or I shouldn’t just sit there.

The part about pushing myself harder than I’d like… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m doing intense workouts all the time or anything like that. But there are times when I keep moving even when I don’t really want to, because stopping makes me feel guilty or uneasy. So I guess it does feel like I have to sometimes. I just have trouble knowing where the line is between normal activity and something that counts as a problem.

Like I plan my whole day around not really eating very much, even if I don’t say it that way.\n\nUsually I’ll have coffee before studio, because it kind of gets me through the morning and makes it easier not to think about food. And then if I’m around people, like at the café or if someone’s noticing, I might have a few bites of something so it doesn’t become a whole conversation. But I’m pretty careful. I avoid bread, and anything oily or greasy, because it just feels like… I don’t know, like it would undo the control I’ve been working so hard to keep. \n\nSo yes, I guess I have changed how I eat. I don’t know if I’d call it “trying to lose weight” exactly, but I’m definitely trying to keep things controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of gaining weight or do you do anything to prevent yourself from gaining weight, even if you’re already at a low weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, I don’t like saying it that bluntly, because it sounds irrational when I hear it out loud, but yes. Even the idea of gaining, like, a pound makes me panic. Not just uncomfortable—panic, like I’ve done something wrong or I’ve let everything slip.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because I know people say, “One dinner doesn’t change anything,” but in my head it doesn’t feel like that. It’s more that if I answer “yes” to “even once,” then it feels like I’m putting something very specific on the record that I’m not actually sure is true. And if I answer “no,” I worry you’ll think I’m saying none of the weight-control stuff is an issue, which isn’t exactly what I mean either.\n\nFor vomiting or laxatives specifically, though… I don’t have a clear instance I can give you. I’m not sitting here thinking of a time and just refusing to say it. I just don’t know that there’s anything there that fits what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise specifically to control your weight or shape, even if it means pushing yourself harder than you'd like or feeling like you have to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know that probably sounds like I’m circling around it again, but exercise is harder for me to separate out than the other things, because it can look normal from the outside. Like, if I walk more or move around because I feel uncomfortable after eating, I can tell myself that’s just being restless or needing to clear my head.\n\nBut when you say “specifically to control weight or shape,” I guess… sometimes it feels connected to that, yes, or at least to feeling less panicked about my body. I don’t always think, “I am exercising to change my weight,” in such a clear sentence in my head. It’s more like this pressure, like I should be doing something, or I shouldn’t just sit there.\n\nThe part about pushing myself harder than I’d like… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m doing intense workouts all the time or anything like that. But there are times when I keep moving even when I don’t really want to, because stopping makes me feel guilty or uneasy. So I guess it does feel like I have to sometimes. I just have trouble knowing where the line is between normal activity and something that counts as a problem.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel driven to exercise even when you’re tired or don’t feel like it, because not doing it makes you anxious about your weight or shape?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that’s probably true, even if I don’t always call it that in my head.

It’s not always like, “I must exercise right now or I’ll gain weight,” in those exact words. Sometimes it’s more this awful feeling that builds up if I’m sitting still, especially if I’ve eaten something that feels too normal or too heavy. Like if I had a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I start thinking about it almost immediately, and then the idea of not doing anything feels unbearable.

And I know it probably sounds dramatic, but even the idea of gaining a pound can make me panic. Not just mildly uncomfortable—like actual panic, like I’ve ruined something, or everyone will somehow be able to see it on me. Like they’ll look at me and know I lost my discipline. So when I’m tired, or I don’t really want to move, there’s still this pressure in my head saying I should, because otherwise I’ll feel worse.

I don’t know if that answers it exactly. I don’t always want to exercise, but not doing it can make me feel really anxious about my body and my weight, yes.

I’m Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what’s been going on that brought you in today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… so I’m here because student health basically wouldn’t clear me for the youth workshops I’m supposed to do. They said my weight is too low, and that it’s been dropping, like not just a one-time thing. They seemed pretty concerned about the number.\n\nI guess I don’t really see it the same way? I mean, I know they’re the medical people, but from my side it just feels like… when I’m really focused and busy, things shift. I’ve had a lot going on with school stuff and the workshops, and I get into a routine, and then suddenly everyone is acting like it’s this huge problem. So they sent me here, and I’m trying to cooperate, but I don’t know. It feels a little bigger to them than it does to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally trying to lose weight or change your eating habits recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… intentionally is a hard word, because I don’t wake up thinking, like, “I need to lose X amount.” It’s more that I’ve gotten really structured about food, I guess. Like I plan my whole day around not really eating very much, even if I don’t say it that way.\n\nUsually I’ll have coffee before studio, because it kind of gets me through the morning and makes it easier not to think about food. And then if I’m around people, like at the café or if someone’s noticing, I might have a few bites of something so it doesn’t become a whole conversation. But I’m pretty careful. I avoid bread, and anything oily or greasy, because it just feels like… I don’t know, like it would undo the control I’ve been working so hard to keep. \n\nSo yes, I guess I have changed how I eat. I don’t know if I’d call it “trying to lose weight” exactly, but I’m definitely trying to keep things controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of gaining weight or do you do anything to prevent yourself from gaining weight, even if you’re already at a low weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, I don’t like saying it that bluntly, because it sounds irrational when I hear it out loud, but yes. Even the idea of gaining, like, a pound makes me panic. Not just uncomfortable—panic, like I’ve done something wrong or I’ve let everything slip.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because I know people say, “One dinner doesn’t change anything,” but in my head it doesn’t feel like that. If I eat what someone else would call a normal dinner, or if there’s butter in something, or it seems too heavy, I start thinking everyone will be able to see it. Like it will show immediately, somehow, and they’ll know I lost discipline. And then I feel like I have to make sure it doesn’t happen again.\n\nSo I guess, yes, I do things to prevent it. I keep things really controlled because the alternative feels… terrifying, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit, used laxatives, or exercised excessively to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way, because I know what you’re asking, but I also feel like if I say anything wrong it’s going to sound worse than I mean it.\n\nI don’t have, like… a whole routine where I’m doing that, if that’s what you mean. I’m not sure. I guess I think about “making up for” food a lot, but the specific things you listed… I don’t know. I don’t really want to say something and have it turned into this big label or have everyone watching me even more closely.\n\nCan you maybe explain what you mean by “excessively”? Because I feel like people define that differently. Like, some people think any exercise is too much if you’re already thin, and other people act like it’s healthy to be super active, so I get kind of confused about where the line is supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve ever made yourself vomit, used laxatives, or exercised a lot specifically to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that without feeling like I’m either admitting to something bigger than it is or pretending there’s nothing there. Like, the vomiting and laxatives part—I don’t really have something clear to say about that. It’s not like I can point to a pattern and be like, “Yes, I do this exact thing.” I know people do, and I know why you’re asking, but I don’t think I can honestly give you some definite answer there beyond… I don’t know, I’m not sure.\n\nWith exercise, that’s the part that gets confusing for me, because I don’t always know what counts as “a lot.” Sometimes I feel like if I move around more after eating, or if I feel like I need to be active because I’ve had too much, then that probably matters, but I also tell myself it’s just normal, or it’s just because I’m restless, or because sitting still makes me feel worse. So I’m kind of stuck between knowing there’s something about it that’s connected to my weight and shape, and also not wanting to call it “excessive” because that makes it sound so deliberate and extreme.\n\nI guess the most honest thing I can say is that I do think about controlling it a lot, but I don’t know how to categorize the specific behaviors you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know that I can give you a clear yes. I’m not trying to be difficult, I just don’t want to say something inaccurate and then have it mean more than I meant.\n\nWith vomiting or laxatives specifically, I don’t have some definite thing I can point to and say, “Yes, I do that to control my weight.” It’s more that the whole topic makes me nervous, because I know why you’re asking, and I know it sounds like there should be a simple answer, but for me it feels… blurry, I guess.\n\nI don’t think I can honestly say yes to those specific things. They said my weight is too low, and that it’s been dropping, like not just a one-time thing. They seemed pretty concerned about the number.\n\nI guess I don’t really see it the same way? I mean, I know they’re the medical people, but from my side it just feels like… when I’m really focused and busy, things shift. I’ve had a lot going on with school stuff and the workshops, and I get into a routine, and then suddenly everyone is acting like it’s this huge problem. So they sent me here, and I’m trying to cooperate, but I don’t know. It feels a little bigger to them than it does to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally trying to lose weight or change your eating habits recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… intentionally is a hard word, because I don’t wake up thinking, like, “I need to lose X amount.” It’s more that I’ve gotten really structured about food, I guess. Like I plan my whole day around not really eating very much, even if I don’t say it that way.\n\nUsually I’ll have coffee before studio, because it kind of gets me through the morning and makes it easier not to think about food. And then if I’m around people, like at the café or if someone’s noticing, I might have a few bites of something so it doesn’t become a whole conversation. But I’m pretty careful. I avoid bread, and anything oily or greasy, because it just feels like… I don’t know, like it would undo the control I’ve been working so hard to keep. \n\nSo yes, I guess I have changed how I eat. I don’t know if I’d call it “trying to lose weight” exactly, but I’m definitely trying to keep things controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of gaining weight or do you do anything to prevent yourself from gaining weight, even if you’re already at a low weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, I don’t like saying it that bluntly, because it sounds irrational when I hear it out loud, but yes. Even the idea of gaining, like, a pound makes me panic. Not just uncomfortable—panic, like I’ve done something wrong or I’ve let everything slip.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because I know people say, “One dinner doesn’t change anything,” but in my head it doesn’t feel like that. If I eat what someone else would call a normal dinner, or if there’s butter in something, or it seems too heavy, I start thinking everyone will be able to see it. Like it will show immediately, somehow, and they’ll know I lost discipline. And then I feel like I have to make sure it doesn’t happen again.\n\nSo I guess, yes, I do things to prevent it. I keep things really controlled because the alternative feels… terrifying, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit, used laxatives, or exercised excessively to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way, because I know what you’re asking, but I also feel like if I say anything wrong it’s going to sound worse than I mean it.\n\nI don’t have, like… a whole routine where I’m doing that, if that’s what you mean. I’m not sure. I guess I think about “making up for” food a lot, but the specific things you listed… I don’t know. I don’t really want to say something and have it turned into this big label or have everyone watching me even more closely.\n\nCan you maybe explain what you mean by “excessively”? Because I feel like people define that differently. Like, some people think any exercise is too much if you’re already thin, and other people act like it’s healthy to be super active, so I get kind of confused about where the line is supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve ever made yourself vomit, used laxatives, or exercised a lot specifically to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that without feeling like I’m either admitting to something bigger than it is or pretending there’s nothing there. Like, the vomiting and laxatives part—I don’t really have something clear to say about that. It’s not like I can point to a pattern and be like, “Yes, I do this exact thing.” I know people do, and I know why you’re asking, but I don’t think I can honestly give you some definite answer there beyond… I don’t know, I’m not sure.\n\nWith exercise, that’s the part that gets confusing for me, because I don’t always know what counts as “a lot.” Sometimes I feel like if I move around more after eating, or if I feel like I need to be active because I’ve had too much, then that probably matters, but I also tell myself it’s just normal, or it’s just because I’m restless, or because sitting still makes me feel worse. So I’m kind of stuck between knowing there’s something about it that’s connected to my weight and shape, and also not wanting to call it “excessive” because that makes it sound so deliberate and extreme.\n\nI guess the most honest thing I can say is that I do think about controlling it a lot, but I don’t know how to categorize the specific behaviors you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know that I can give you a clear yes. I’m not trying to be difficult, I just don’t want to say something inaccurate and then have it mean more than I meant.\n\nWith vomiting or laxatives specifically, I don’t have some definite thing I can point to and say, “Yes, I do that to control my weight.” It’s more that the whole topic makes me nervous, because I know why you’re asking, and I know it sounds like there should be a simple answer, but for me it feels… blurry, I guess.\n\nI don’t think I can honestly say yes to those specific things. But I also feel weird saying “no” too confidently, because then it sounds like everything is fine or like I’m not trying to control things in other ways. For vomiting or laxatives, though, I’m not sure there’s anything clear for me to report.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives to control your weight or shape, even once?\n\nPatient: Even once… I mean, I’m trying to think of it that literally, and I don’t think I can tell you, “Yes, I did that to control my weight or shape.” Like, not in a way that I can clearly identify and say that was what was happening.\n\nI know that sounds evasive, but I’m not trying to dodge it. It’s more that if I answer “yes” to “even once,” then it feels like I’m putting something very specific on the record that I’m not actually sure is true. And if I answer “no,” I worry you’ll think I’m saying none of the weight-control stuff is an issue, which isn’t exactly what I mean either.\n\nFor vomiting or laxatives specifically, though… I don’t have a clear instance I can give you. I’m not sitting here thinking of a time and just refusing to say it. I just don’t know that there’s anything there that fits what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise specifically to control your weight or shape, even if it means pushing yourself harder than you'd like or feeling like you have to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know that probably sounds like I’m circling around it again, but exercise is harder for me to separate out than the other things, because it can look normal from the outside. Like, if I walk more or move around because I feel uncomfortable after eating, I can tell myself that’s just being restless or needing to clear my head.\n\nBut when you say “specifically to control weight or shape,” I guess… sometimes it feels connected to that, yes, or at least to feeling less panicked about my body. I don’t always think, “I am exercising to change my weight,” in such a clear sentence in my head. It’s more like this pressure, like I should be doing something, or I shouldn’t just sit there.\n\nThe part about pushing myself harder than I’d like… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m doing intense workouts all the time or anything like that. But there are times when I keep moving even when I don’t really want to, because stopping makes me feel guilty or uneasy. So I guess it does feel like I have to sometimes. I just have trouble knowing where the line is between normal activity and something that counts as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel driven to exercise even when you’re tired or don’t feel like it, because not doing it makes you anxious about your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably true, even if I don’t always call it that in my head.\n\nIt’s not always like, “I must exercise right now or I’ll gain weight,” in those exact words. Sometimes it’s more this awful feeling that builds up if I’m sitting still, especially if I’ve eaten something that feels too normal or too heavy. Like if I had a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I start thinking about it almost immediately, and then the idea of not doing anything feels unbearable.\n\nAnd I know it probably sounds dramatic, but even the idea of gaining a pound can make me panic. Not just mildly uncomfortable—like actual panic, like I’ve ruined something, or everyone will somehow be able to see it on me. Like they’ll look at me and know I lost my discipline. So when I’m tired, or I don’t really want to move, there’s still this pressure in my head saying I should, because otherwise I’ll feel worse.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it exactly. I don’t always want to exercise, but not doing it can make me feel really anxious about my body and my weight, yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle, like missing periods or them stopping altogether?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure how to answer that. I mean… I know you’re asking whether I’ve missed periods or whether they’ve stopped, and I don’t want to give you a false answer just because I feel like I’m supposed to know.\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it in a very exact way, if that makes sense. Like, I know some people know the dates and can say exactly how regular they are, but I don’t have that clearly in my head. So I can’t confidently say, “Yes, they stopped,” or “Yes, I missed this many.” I just… I don’t know.\n\nI haven’t noticed a really obvious change that I could point to, like something dramatic where I thought, oh, that’s definitely different. But I also feel a little embarrassed saying that because maybe I should be paying closer attention. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very useful answer.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like, the vomiting and laxatives part—I don’t really have something clear to say about that. It’s not like I can point to a pattern and be like, “Yes, I do this exact thing.” I know people do, and I know why you’re asking, but I don’t think I can honestly give you some definite answer there beyond… I don’t know, I’m not sure.\n\nWith exercise, that’s the part that gets confusing for me, because I don’t always know what counts as “a lot.” Sometimes I feel like if I move around more after eating, or if I feel like I need to be active because I’ve had too much, then that probably matters, but I also tell myself it’s just normal, or it’s just because I’m restless, or because sitting still makes me feel worse. So I’m kind of stuck between knowing there’s something about it that’s connected to my weight and shape, and also not wanting to call it “excessive” because that makes it sound so deliberate and extreme.\n\nI guess the most honest thing I can say is that I do think about controlling it a lot, but I don’t know how to categorize the specific behaviors you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know that I can give you a clear yes. I’m not trying to be difficult, I just don’t want to say something inaccurate and then have it mean more than I meant.\n\nWith vomiting or laxatives specifically, I don’t have some definite thing I can point to and say, “Yes, I do that to control my weight.” It’s more that the whole topic makes me nervous, because I know why you’re asking, and I know it sounds like there should be a simple answer, but for me it feels… blurry, I guess.\n\nI don’t think I can honestly say yes to those specific things. But I also feel weird saying “no” too confidently, because then it sounds like everything is fine or like I’m not trying to control things in other ways. For vomiting or laxatives, though, I’m not sure there’s anything clear for me to report.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives to control your weight or shape, even once?\n\nPatient: Even once… I mean, I’m trying to think of it that literally, and I don’t think I can tell you, “Yes, I did that to control my weight or shape.” Like, not in a way that I can clearly identify and say that was what was happening.\n\nI know that sounds evasive, but I’m not trying to dodge it. It’s more that if I answer “yes” to “even once,” then it feels like I’m putting something very specific on the record that I’m not actually sure is true. And if I answer “no,” I worry you’ll think I’m saying none of the weight-control stuff is an issue, which isn’t exactly what I mean either.\n\nFor vomiting or laxatives specifically, though… I don’t have a clear instance I can give you. I’m not sitting here thinking of a time and just refusing to say it. I just don’t know that there’s anything there that fits what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise specifically to control your weight or shape, even if it means pushing yourself harder than you'd like or feeling like you have to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know that probably sounds like I’m circling around it again, but exercise is harder for me to separate out than the other things, because it can look normal from the outside. Like, if I walk more or move around because I feel uncomfortable after eating, I can tell myself that’s just being restless or needing to clear my head.\n\nBut when you say “specifically to control weight or shape,” I guess… sometimes it feels connected to that, yes, or at least to feeling less panicked about my body. I don’t always think, “I am exercising to change my weight,” in such a clear sentence in my head. It’s more like this pressure, like I should be doing something, or I shouldn’t just sit there.\n\nThe part about pushing myself harder than I’d like… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m doing intense workouts all the time or anything like that. But there are times when I keep moving even when I don’t really want to, because stopping makes me feel guilty or uneasy. So I guess it does feel like I have to sometimes. I just have trouble knowing where the line is between normal activity and something that counts as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel driven to exercise even when you’re tired or don’t feel like it, because not doing it makes you anxious about your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably true, even if I don’t always call it that in my head.\n\nIt’s not always like, “I must exercise right now or I’ll gain weight,” in those exact words. Sometimes it’s more this awful feeling that builds up if I’m sitting still, especially if I’ve eaten something that feels too normal or too heavy. Like if I had a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I start thinking about it almost immediately, and then the idea of not doing anything feels unbearable.\n\nAnd I know it probably sounds dramatic, but even the idea of gaining a pound can make me panic. Not just mildly uncomfortable—like actual panic, like I’ve ruined something, or everyone will somehow be able to see it on me. Like they’ll look at me and know I lost my discipline. So when I’m tired, or I don’t really want to move, there’s still this pressure in my head saying I should, because otherwise I’ll feel worse.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it exactly. I don’t always want to exercise, but not doing it can make me feel really anxious about my body and my weight, yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle, like missing periods or them stopping altogether?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure how to answer that. I mean… I know you’re asking whether I’ve missed periods or whether they’ve stopped, and I don’t want to give you a false answer just because I feel like I’m supposed to know.\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it in a very exact way, if that makes sense. Like, I know some people know the dates and can say exactly how regular they are, but I don’t have that clearly in my head. So I can’t confidently say, “Yes, they stopped,” or “Yes, I missed this many.” I just… I don’t know.\n\nI haven’t noticed a really obvious change that I could point to, like something dramatic where I thought, oh, that’s definitely different. But I also feel a little embarrassed saying that because maybe I should be paying closer attention. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very useful answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you gone three months or more without having a period, even though you are not pregnant and it's not due to another medical condition?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say yes to that. I mean, not confidently.\n\nThree months is a very specific amount of time, and I haven’t kept track closely enough to know that for sure. I’m not pregnant, as far as I know, and I’m not aware of another medical reason, but I also don’t want to answer like I have records or exact dates when I really don’t.\n\nI don’t remember there being a clear stretch where I thought, “Oh, it’s been three whole months without a period.” But I also haven’t been writing it down, so there’s this uncomfortable space where I’m not sure if I would have noticed properly. I know that probably isn’t as helpful as a definite yes or no, but the honest answer is that I don’t know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t always want to exercise, but not doing it can make me feel really anxious about my body and my weight, yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle, like missing periods or them stopping altogether?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure how to answer that. I mean… I know you’re asking whether I’ve missed periods or whether they’ve stopped, and I don’t want to give you a false answer just because I feel like I’m supposed to know.\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it in a very exact way, if that makes sense. Like, I know some people know the dates and can say exactly how regular they are, but I don’t have that clearly in my head. So I can’t confidently say, “Yes, they stopped,” or “Yes, I missed this many.” I just… I don’t know.\n\nI haven’t noticed a really obvious change that I could point to, like something dramatic where I thought, oh, that’s definitely different. But I also feel a little embarrassed saying that because maybe I should be paying closer attention. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very useful answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you gone three months or more without having a period, even though you are not pregnant and it's not due to another medical condition?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say yes to that. I mean, not confidently.\n\nThree months is a very specific amount of time, and I haven’t kept track closely enough to know that for sure. I’m not pregnant, as far as I know, and I’m not aware of another medical reason, but I also don’t want to answer like I have records or exact dates when I really don’t.\n\nI don’t remember there being a clear stretch where I thought, “Oh, it’s been three whole months without a period.” But I also haven’t been writing it down, so there’s this uncomfortable space where I’m not sure if I would have noticed properly. I know that probably isn’t as helpful as a definite yes or no, but the honest answer is that I don’t know.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say the weight loss you've experienced was something you were actively trying to achieve, or did it happen more as a result of your daily routine and focus on controlling your eating without necessarily aiming for further weight loss?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 19 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Restrictive_Intake\nmanifestation: \"I plan my whole day around not really eating—coffee before studio, maybe a few bites of something from the café if people are watching, and I avoid bread or anything oily because it feels like it would undo the control I’ve worked for.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… it’s hard to separate those things, honestly. Like I don’t always wake up thinking, “I need to lose more weight today,” in such a clear, direct way. It’s more that my whole day sort of gets organized around not really eating, and then the weight loss just becomes tied up with that.

Like before studio I’ll usually just have coffee, because if I eat before I go, I feel like I’ve already messed up the day somehow. And then if people are around, I might have a few bites of something from the café so it looks normal, or so no one asks too many questions, but I’m still thinking the whole time about how much it is and whether I can get away with not finishing it.

I avoid bread almost automatically now. And anything oily, or with butter, or anything that feels heavy. It feels like if I eat those things, it would undo all the control I’ve been working so hard to keep. So I guess… yes, I am controlling it on purpose. But it doesn’t always feel like I’m actively chasing a number every second. It’s more like the routine is built around keeping control, and not eating feels safer than eating.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.00, F50.1, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I’m Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what’s been going on that brought you in today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… so I’m here because student health basically wouldn’t clear me for the youth workshops I’m supposed to do. They said my weight is too low, and that it’s been dropping, like not just a one-time thing. They seemed pretty concerned about the number.\n\nI guess I don’t really see it the same way? I mean, I know they’re the medical people, but from my side it just feels like… when I’m really focused and busy, things shift. I’ve had a lot going on with school stuff and the workshops, and I get into a routine, and then suddenly everyone is acting like it’s this huge problem. So they sent me here, and I’m trying to cooperate, but I don’t know. It feels a little bigger to them than it does to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally trying to lose weight or change your eating habits recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… intentionally is a hard word, because I don’t wake up thinking, like, “I need to lose X amount.” It’s more that I’ve gotten really structured about food, I guess. Like I plan my whole day around not really eating very much, even if I don’t say it that way.\n\nUsually I’ll have coffee before studio, because it kind of gets me through the morning and makes it easier not to think about food. And then if I’m around people, like at the café or if someone’s noticing, I might have a few bites of something so it doesn’t become a whole conversation. But I’m pretty careful. I avoid bread, and anything oily or greasy, because it just feels like… I don’t know, like it would undo the control I’ve been working so hard to keep. \n\nSo yes, I guess I have changed how I eat. I don’t know if I’d call it “trying to lose weight” exactly, but I’m definitely trying to keep things controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of gaining weight or do you do anything to prevent yourself from gaining weight, even if you’re already at a low weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, I don’t like saying it that bluntly, because it sounds irrational when I hear it out loud, but yes. Even the idea of gaining, like, a pound makes me panic. Not just uncomfortable—panic, like I’ve done something wrong or I’ve let everything slip.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because I know people say, “One dinner doesn’t change anything,” but in my head it doesn’t feel like that. If I eat what someone else would call a normal dinner, or if there’s butter in something, or it seems too heavy, I start thinking everyone will be able to see it. Like it will show immediately, somehow, and they’ll know I lost discipline. And then I feel like I have to make sure it doesn’t happen again.\n\nSo I guess, yes, I do things to prevent it. I keep things really controlled because the alternative feels… terrifying, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit, used laxatives, or exercised excessively to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way, because I know what you’re asking, but I also feel like if I say anything wrong it’s going to sound worse than I mean it.\n\nI don’t have, like… a whole routine where I’m doing that, if that’s what you mean. I’m not sure. I guess I think about “making up for” food a lot, but the specific things you listed… I don’t know. I don’t really want to say something and have it turned into this big label or have everyone watching me even more closely.\n\nCan you maybe explain what you mean by “excessively”? Because I feel like people define that differently. Like, some people think any exercise is too much if you’re already thin, and other people act like it’s healthy to be super active, so I get kind of confused about where the line is supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve ever made yourself vomit, used laxatives, or exercised a lot specifically to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that without feeling like I’m either admitting to something bigger than it is or pretending there’s nothing there. Like, the vomiting and laxatives part—I don’t really have something clear to say about that. It’s not like I can point to a pattern and be like, “Yes, I do this exact thing.” I know people do, and I know why you’re asking, but I don’t think I can honestly give you some definite answer there beyond… I don’t know, I’m not sure.\n\nWith exercise, that’s the part that gets confusing for me, because I don’t always know what counts as “a lot.” Sometimes I feel like if I move around more after eating, or if I feel like I need to be active because I’ve had too much, then that probably matters, but I also tell myself it’s just normal, or it’s just because I’m restless, or because sitting still makes me feel worse. So I’m kind of stuck between knowing there’s something about it that’s connected to my weight and shape, and also not wanting to call it “excessive” because that makes it sound so deliberate and extreme.\n\nI guess the most honest thing I can say is that I do think about controlling it a lot, but I don’t know how to categorize the specific behaviors you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know that I can give you a clear yes. I’m not trying to be difficult, I just don’t want to say something inaccurate and then have it mean more than I meant.\n\nWith vomiting or laxatives specifically, I don’t have some definite thing I can point to and say, “Yes, I do that to control my weight.” It’s more that the whole topic makes me nervous, because I know why you’re asking, and I know it sounds like there should be a simple answer, but for me it feels… blurry, I guess.\n\nI don’t think I can honestly say yes to those specific things. But I also feel weird saying “no” too confidently, because then it sounds like everything is fine or like I’m not trying to control things in other ways. For vomiting or laxatives, though, I’m not sure there’s anything clear for me to report.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives to control your weight or shape, even once?\n\nPatient: Even once… I mean, I’m trying to think of it that literally, and I don’t think I can tell you, “Yes, I did that to control my weight or shape.” Like, not in a way that I can clearly identify and say that was what was happening.\n\nI know that sounds evasive, but I’m not trying to dodge it. It’s more that if I answer “yes” to “even once,” then it feels like I’m putting something very specific on the record that I’m not actually sure is true. And if I answer “no,” I worry you’ll think I’m saying none of the weight-control stuff is an issue, which isn’t exactly what I mean either.\n\nFor vomiting or laxatives specifically, though… I don’t have a clear instance I can give you. I’m not sitting here thinking of a time and just refusing to say it. I just don’t know that there’s anything there that fits what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise specifically to control your weight or shape, even if it means pushing yourself harder than you'd like or feeling like you have to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know that probably sounds like I’m circling around it again, but exercise is harder for me to separate out than the other things, because it can look normal from the outside. Like, if I walk more or move around because I feel uncomfortable after eating, I can tell myself that’s just being restless or needing to clear my head.\n\nBut when you say “specifically to control weight or shape,” I guess… sometimes it feels connected to that, yes, or at least to feeling less panicked about my body. I don’t always think, “I am exercising to change my weight,” in such a clear sentence in my head. It’s more like this pressure, like I should be doing something, or I shouldn’t just sit there.\n\nThe part about pushing myself harder than I’d like… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m doing intense workouts all the time or anything like that. But there are times when I keep moving even when I don’t really want to, because stopping makes me feel guilty or uneasy. So I guess it does feel like I have to sometimes. I just have trouble knowing where the line is between normal activity and something that counts as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel driven to exercise even when you’re tired or don’t feel like it, because not doing it makes you anxious about your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably true, even if I don’t always call it that in my head.\n\nIt’s not always like, “I must exercise right now or I’ll gain weight,” in those exact words. Sometimes it’s more this awful feeling that builds up if I’m sitting still, especially if I’ve eaten something that feels too normal or too heavy. Like if I had a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I start thinking about it almost immediately, and then the idea of not doing anything feels unbearable.\n\nAnd I know it probably sounds dramatic, but even the idea of gaining a pound can make me panic. Not just mildly uncomfortable—like actual panic, like I’ve ruined something, or everyone will somehow be able to see it on me. Like they’ll look at me and know I lost my discipline. So when I’m tired, or I don’t really want to move, there’s still this pressure in my head saying I should, because otherwise I’ll feel worse.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it exactly. I don’t always want to exercise, but not doing it can make me feel really anxious about my body and my weight, yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle, like missing periods or them stopping altogether?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure how to answer that. I’m sorry, I know you asked it again because it matters, but I still can’t really give you a clean yes or no. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know how to answer that in a clean way. I mean… I don’t think I have anything definite to give you there.

When you say “made myself vomit,” do you mean like deliberately going to the bathroom and doing it on purpose after eating? Because I don’t have some clear pattern like that that I can point to. And with laxatives, I’m not sure—like, I know what you mean generally, but I don’t have a specific thing where I can say, yes, I used them to change my weight or shape.

I feel awkward saying it because I know you’re asking very directly, and I don’t want to dodge it, but I also don’t want to invent certainty where I don’t have it. The main thing for me has been more about trying not to eat much in the first place, rather than eating and then doing something afterward. So… no, not that I can clearly say.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.00, F50.01, F50.1, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I’m Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what’s been going on that brought you in today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… so I’m here because student health basically wouldn’t clear me for the youth workshops I’m supposed to do. They said my weight is too low, and that it’s been dropping, like not just a one-time thing. They seemed pretty concerned about the number.\n\nI guess I don’t really see it the same way? I mean, I know they’re the medical people, but from my side it just feels like… when I’m really focused and busy, things shift. I’ve had a lot going on with school stuff and the workshops, and I get into a routine, and then suddenly everyone is acting like it’s this huge problem. So they sent me here, and I’m trying to cooperate, but I don’t know. It feels a little bigger to them than it does to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally trying to lose weight or change your eating habits recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… intentionally is a hard word, because I don’t wake up thinking, like, “I need to lose X amount.” It’s more that I’ve gotten really structured about food, I guess. Like I plan my whole day around not really eating very much, even if I don’t say it that way.\n\nUsually I’ll have coffee before studio, because it kind of gets me through the morning and makes it easier not to think about food. And then if I’m around people, like at the café or if someone’s noticing, I might have a few bites of something so it doesn’t become a whole conversation. But I’m pretty careful. I avoid bread, and anything oily or greasy, because it just feels like… I don’t know, like it would undo the control I’ve been working so hard to keep. \n\nSo yes, I guess I have changed how I eat. I don’t know if I’d call it “trying to lose weight” exactly, but I’m definitely trying to keep things controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of gaining weight or do you do anything to prevent yourself from gaining weight, even if you’re already at a low weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, I don’t like saying it that bluntly, because it sounds irrational when I hear it out loud, but yes. Even the idea of gaining, like, a pound makes me panic. Not just uncomfortable—panic, like I’ve done something wrong or I’ve let everything slip.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because I know people say, “One dinner doesn’t change anything,” but in my head it doesn’t feel like that. If I eat what someone else would call a normal dinner, or if there’s butter in something, or it seems too heavy, I start thinking everyone will be able to see it. Like it will show immediately, somehow, and they’ll know I lost discipline. And then I feel like I have to make sure it doesn’t happen again.\n\nSo I guess, yes, I do things to prevent it. I keep things really controlled because the alternative feels… terrifying, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit, used laxatives, or exercised excessively to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way, because I know what you’re asking, but I also feel like if I say anything wrong it’s going to sound worse than I mean it.\n\nI don’t have, like… a whole routine where I’m doing that, if that’s what you mean. I’m not sure. I guess I think about “making up for” food a lot, but the specific things you listed… I don’t know. I don’t really want to say something and have it turned into this big label or have everyone watching me even more closely.\n\nCan you maybe explain what you mean by “excessively”? Because I feel like people define that differently. Like, some people think any exercise is too much if you’re already thin, and other people act like it’s healthy to be super active, so I get kind of confused about where the line is supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve ever made yourself vomit, used laxatives, or exercised a lot specifically to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that without feeling like I’m either admitting to something bigger than it is or pretending there’s nothing there. Like, the vomiting and laxatives part—I don’t really have something clear to say about that. It’s not like I can point to a pattern and be like, “Yes, I do this exact thing.” I know people do, and I know why you’re asking, but I don’t think I can honestly give you some definite answer there beyond… I don’t know, I’m not sure.\n\nWith exercise, that’s the part that gets confusing for me, because I don’t always know what counts as “a lot.” Sometimes I feel like if I move around more after eating, or if I feel like I need to be active because I’ve had too much, then that probably matters, but I also tell myself it’s just normal, or it’s just because I’m restless, or because sitting still makes me feel worse. So I’m kind of stuck between knowing there’s something about it that’s connected to my weight and shape, and also not wanting to call it “excessive” because that makes it sound so deliberate and extreme.\n\nI guess the most honest thing I can say is that I do think about controlling it a lot, but I don’t know how to categorize the specific behaviors you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know that I can give you a clear yes. I’m not trying to be difficult, I just don’t want to say something inaccurate and then have it mean more than I meant.\n\nWith vomiting or laxatives specifically, I don’t have some definite thing I can point to and say, “Yes, I do that to control my weight.” It’s more that the whole topic makes me nervous, because I know why you’re asking, and I know it sounds like there should be a simple answer, but for me it feels… blurry, I guess.\n\nI don’t think I can honestly say yes to those specific things. But I also feel weird saying “no” too confidently, because then it sounds like everything is fine or like I’m not trying to control things in other ways. For vomiting or laxatives, though, I’m not sure there’s anything clear for me to report.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives to control your weight or shape, even once?\n\nPatient: Even once… I mean, I’m trying to think of it that literally, and I don’t think I can tell you, “Yes, I did that to control my weight or shape.” Like, not in a way that I can clearly identify and say that was what was happening.\n\nI know that sounds evasive, but I’m not trying to dodge it. It’s more that if I answer “yes” to “even once,” then it feels like I’m putting something very specific on the record that I’m not actually sure is true. And if I answer “no,” I worry you’ll think I’m saying none of the weight-control stuff is an issue, which isn’t exactly what I mean either.\n\nFor vomiting or laxatives specifically, though… I don’t have a clear instance I can give you. I’m not sitting here thinking of a time and just refusing to say it. I just don’t know that there’s anything there that fits what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise specifically to control your weight or shape, even if it means pushing yourself harder than you'd like or feeling like you have to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know that probably sounds like I’m circling around it again, but exercise is harder for me to separate out than the other things, because it can look normal from the outside. Like, if I walk more or move around because I feel uncomfortable after eating, I can tell myself that’s just being restless or needing to clear my head.\n\nBut when you say “specifically to control weight or shape,” I guess… sometimes it feels connected to that, yes, or at least to feeling less panicked about my body. I don’t always think, “I am exercising to change my weight,” in such a clear sentence in my head. It’s more like this pressure, like I should be doing something, or I shouldn’t just sit there.\n\nThe part about pushing myself harder than I’d like… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m doing intense workouts all the time or anything like that. But there are times when I keep moving even when I don’t really want to, because stopping makes me feel guilty or uneasy. So I guess it does feel like I have to sometimes. I just have trouble knowing where the line is between normal activity and something that counts as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel driven to exercise even when you’re tired or don’t feel like it, because not doing it makes you anxious about your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably true, even if I don’t always call it that in my head.\n\nIt’s not always like, “I must exercise right now or I’ll gain weight,” in those exact words. Sometimes it’s more this awful feeling that builds up if I’m sitting still, especially if I’ve eaten something that feels too normal or too heavy. Like if I had a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I start thinking about it almost immediately, and then the idea of not doing anything feels unbearable.\n\nAnd I know it probably sounds dramatic, but even the idea of gaining a pound can make me panic. Not just mildly uncomfortable—like actual panic, like I’ve ruined something, or everyone will somehow be able to see it on me. Like they’ll look at me and know I lost my discipline. So when I’m tired, or I don’t really want to move, there’s still this pressure in my head saying I should, because otherwise I’ll feel worse.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it exactly. I don’t always want to exercise, but not doing it can make me feel really anxious about my body and my weight, yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle, like missing periods or them stopping altogether?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure how to answer that. I mean… I know you’re asking whether I’ve missed periods or whether they’ve stopped, and I don’t want to give you a false answer just because I feel like I’m supposed to know.\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it in a very exact way, if that makes sense. Like, I know some people know the dates and can say exactly how regular they are, but I don’t have that clearly in my head. So I can’t confidently say, “Yes, they stopped,” or “Yes, I missed this many.” I just… I don’t know.\n\nI haven’t noticed a really obvious change that I could point to, like something dramatic where I thought, oh, that’s definitely different. But I also feel a little embarrassed saying that because maybe I should be paying closer attention. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very useful answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you gone three months or more without having a period, even though you are not pregnant and it's not due to another medical condition?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say yes to that. I mean, not confidently.\n\nThree months is a very specific amount of time, and I haven’t kept track closely enough to know that for sure. I’m not pregnant, as far as I know, and I’m not aware of another medical reason, but I also don’t want to answer like I have records or exact dates when I really don’t.\n\nI don’t remember there being a clear stretch where I thought, “Oh, it’s been three whole months without a period.” But I also haven’t been writing it down, so there’s this uncomfortable space where I’m not sure if I would have noticed properly. I know that probably isn’t as helpful as a definite yes or no, but the honest answer is that I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the weight loss you've experienced was something you were actively trying to achieve, or did it happen more as a result of your daily routine and focus on controlling your eating without necessarily aiming for further weight loss?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s hard to separate those things, honestly. Like I don’t always wake up thinking, “I need to lose more weight today,” in such a clear, direct way. It’s more that my whole day sort of gets organized around not really eating, and then the weight loss just becomes tied up with that.\n\nLike before studio I’ll usually just have coffee, because if I eat before I go, I feel like I’ve already messed up the day somehow. And then if people are around, I might have a few bites of something from the café so it looks normal, or so no one asks too many questions, but I’m still thinking the whole time about how much it is and whether I can get away with not finishing it.\n\nI avoid bread almost automatically now. And anything oily, or with butter, or anything that feels heavy. It feels like if I eat those things, it would undo all the control I’ve been working so hard to keep. So I guess… yes, I am controlling it on purpose. But it doesn’t always feel like I’m actively chasing a number every second. It’s more like the routine is built around keeping control, and not eating feels safer than eating.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there has ever been a time when you went three months or more without having a menstrual period, even though you were not pregnant and it wasn’t due to another medical condition?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you asked it again because it matters, but I still can’t really give you a clean yes or no. I’ve been trying to think back more carefully, like whether there was ever a season or part of a semester where I realized it had been that long, and I just… I don’t have a clear memory of three full months without one.\n\nI wasn’t pregnant, as far as I know, and I don’t know of another medical reason, but I also wasn’t keeping a calendar or anything. So part of me worries that maybe I’m missing something just because I wasn’t paying attention closely enough. But I don’t remember ever thinking, “It’s been three months,” or feeling certain that it had stopped for that long.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I don’t know, but I can’t recall a definite time when that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives to control your weight or shape, even if only once or a few times?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that in a clean way. I mean… I don’t think I have anything definite to give you there.\n\nWhen you say “made myself vomit,” do you mean like deliberately going to the bathroom and doing it on purpose after eating? Because I don’t have some clear pattern like that that I can point to. And with laxatives, I’m not sure—like, I know what you mean generally, but I don’t have a specific thing where I can say, yes, I used them to change my weight or shape.\n\nI feel awkward saying it because I know you’re asking very directly, and I don’t want to dodge it, but I also don’t want to invent certainty where I don’t have it. So… no, not that I can clearly say.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
